========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have. Most days lately I’ve felt this kind of buoyant, almost lit-up feeling, like everything was finally coming together. I’d be in the studio or out in the garden and it felt like, “Of course, this is all clicking into place, I can do all of it, I can fix this, expand that, make it beautiful, make it work.” It wasn’t just being in a good mood, exactly—it was more charged than that.

But then if someone questioned me—like if they asked whether I really had time for something, or brought up invoices or practical things—I could get really sharp. Much sharper than I meant to be. It felt like they were interrupting something obvious and important, even if they were probably just being reasonable. So yes, unusually up, but also… irritable, definitely, when anyone pushed back on my plans.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that’s a good way to put it. It wasn’t just a passing good mood, like having a nice morning or finishing a painting and feeling pleased. It was most days, and it would take over the whole day, really—this buoyant, lit-up feeling, like I had suddenly stepped into the right current and everything was moving with me.

I’d be in the studio and feel like every idea connected to the next one, and then I’d go into the garden and feel the same thing there, like the studio and the garden were both finally clicking into place and I could see exactly what needed to happen. It felt obvious to me, almost embarrassingly obvious, that I should be doing all these things at once.

But if someone questioned it—if they asked whether I had time, or brought up invoices, or said something practical like, “Maybe wait on that,” I could snap. Sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. In the moment it felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, and then afterward I’d think, “Why did I say it like that?” So yes, elevated, but also irritable when people pushed against what I thought was the plan.

I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. But… yes, it has caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at first.

My illustration work has really suffered. I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. And that’s not like me, or at least not like I want to be. Colleagues have started checking in on me, asking where things are, whether I’m okay, whether I need help getting back on track. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.

And the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan. Now the mortgage is at risk, and I’m having to face that my mood and all this activity haven’t just been “creative momentum.” It’s affected my responsibilities in a very real way.

space for them to answer.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I would sit down to finish a plate for a contract and then a bird at the window, a hardware catalog, or a half-formed idea for a workshop would pull me completely off course.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I came in because I’ve been taking on far more projects, workshops, studio improvements, and client calls than I can realistically manage, and now the deadlines and the mortgage are starting to close in on me.\n- key: S041\n  name: Painful_Consequence_Behavior\n  description: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.\n  manifestation: I bought a large-format printer, specialty papers, lighting, and renovation materials as if the money would sort itself out once the big breakthrough landed, and only later did I see how close I’d put the cottage-studio to trouble.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: That certainty about clients stealing my work only showed up during this keyed-up stretch with the studio; before this, I might worry about contracts, but I didn’t believe people were plotting against me. It wasn’t just a passing good mood, like having a nice morning or finishing a painting and feeling pleased. It was most days, and it would take over the whole day, really—this buoyant, lit-up feeling, like I had suddenly stepped into the right current and everything was moving with me.\n\nI’d be in the studio and feel like every idea connected to the next one, and then I’d go into the garden and feel the same thing there, like the studio and the garden were both finally clicking into place and I could see exactly what needed to happen. It felt obvious to me, almost embarrassingly obvious, that I should be doing all these things at once.\n\nBut if someone questioned it—if they asked whether I had time, or brought up invoices, or said something practical like, “Maybe wait on that,” I could snap. Sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. In the moment it felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, and then afterward I’d think, “Why did I say it like that?” So yes, elevated, but also irritable when people pushed against what I thought was the plan.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time recently when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy or activity, lasted for a full week or more without letting up?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than a week. It’s hard to pin down exactly because it didn’t feel like an “episode” at first, it felt like I was finally getting traction. But looking back, this has really been going on for about three months now—since I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio.\n\nThat was sort of the turning point, I think. Once that happened, everything started feeling urgent and possible at the same time, like I had to keep moving and keep building and keep taking things on to make it all work. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way since then. There might be quieter hours here and there, but the overall mood and energy—being so driven, so keyed into everything—has been there pretty consistently for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to be hospitalized or felt that your mood or behavior caused serious problems in your daily life, like work, relationships, or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. But… yes, it has caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nMy illustration work has really suffered. I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. And that’s not like me, or at least not like I want to be. Colleagues have started checking in on me, asking where things are, whether I’m okay, whether I need help getting back on track. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.\n\nAnd the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan. Now the mortgage is at risk, and I’m having to face that my mood and all this activity haven’t just been “creative momentum.” It’s affected my responsibilities in a very real way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any periods where your mood was elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in energy or activity, that lasted for at least four consecutive days and was different enough from your usual behavior that other people could clearly see it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, people could see it. I mean, I don’t think I realized how visible it was at the time, because from inside it felt… wonderful at first, honestly. Like I was unusually buoyant, lit from underneath somehow. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden and it all felt like it was finally coming together, as if both places were part of one clear plan and I could suddenly see what needed doing.

But it wasn’t just feeling pleased or inspired. It was most of the day, most days, and I was much more energized and driven than I usually am. And if anyone questioned me—if they asked something practical, like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or if a plan really made sense—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I’m comfortable admitting. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, like they just couldn’t see what I could see.

So yes, it was different enough that other people noticed. I think they saw both sides of it—the brightness and the irritability.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I can picture it pretty clearly, actually. It wasn’t just one of my normal creative spells where I get absorbed in a piece and lose track of time for an afternoon. This felt bigger than that, and more… charged.

Most days I had this buoyant feeling, like I was lit up from the inside. I’d walk between the studio and the garden and feel like, finally, finally, everything was clicking into place. The cottage, the studio, the planting, the work—it all seemed connected in this very obvious way, like there was a plan unfolding and I was the only one who could really see the whole shape of it.

And I was much more intense than I usually am. I know I can get enthusiastic about projects, but this was different. If someone asked a practical question—like whether I’d handled invoices, or whether a plan was actually affordable, or even just tried to slow me down—I could snap at them. Sharply. Not just feel annoyed, but answer in this cutting way, like they were being foolish for not understanding what I was trying to do.

At the time I thought I was just energized and focused, but looking back, yes, it was a very clear change from my usual creative bursts.

Yes… yes, I can. It’s hard for me to put exact borders around it, because at the time I wasn’t thinking, “this is day one, this is day seven,” but there was definitely a stretch where, most days, I felt much more elevated than I normally do.

Yes, I can think of a time like that. I know I’ve sort of circled around it already, but it really does stand out because it wasn’t just me being in a good working rhythm or getting excited about a project.

It’s hard to pin down exactly because it didn’t feel like an “episode” at first, it felt like I was finally getting traction. But looking back, this has really been going on for about three months now—since I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio.\n\nThat was sort of the turning point, I think. Once that happened, everything started feeling urgent and possible at the same time, like I had to keep moving and keep building and keep taking things on to make it all work. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way since then. There might be quieter hours here and there, but the overall mood and energy—being so driven, so keyed into everything—has been there pretty consistently for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to be hospitalized or felt that your mood or behavior caused serious problems in your daily life, like work, relationships, or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.\n\nAnd the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan. Now the mortgage is at risk, and I’m having to face that my mood and all this activity haven’t just been “creative momentum.” It’s affected my responsibilities in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your mood was elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in energy or activity, that lasted for at least four consecutive days and was different enough from your usual behavior that other people could clearly see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, people could see it. I mean, I don’t think I realized how visible it was at the time, because from inside it felt… wonderful at first, honestly. Like I was unusually buoyant, lit from underneath somehow. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden and it all felt like it was finally coming together, as if both places were part of one clear plan and I could suddenly see what needed doing.\n\nBut it wasn’t just feeling pleased or inspired. It was most of the day, most days, and I was much more energized and driven than I usually am. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I didn’t end up in hospital, but it did start to affect real things in a way I couldn’t just brush off as “being busy” or “being inspired.”

My illustration deadlines slipped, which is very unlike me in that particular way. I might fuss over work or take too long perfecting something, but this was different—I was overcommitting, saying yes to things, starting plans, spending time and energy on all these connected ideas, and then the actual work I was meant to deliver wasn’t getting finished properly or on time.

Colleagues started checking in on me, not just casually, but in that careful way people do when they’re trying not to sound alarmed. And financially… that’s the part I feel most ashamed about. I spent money I really needed to keep the studio secure, because at the time it felt justified, like it was all part of building something important. But now the mortgage is at risk, and that’s frightening. So yes, it wasn’t just a mood. It spilled over into my functioning and other people had to start paying attention.

But now the mortgage is at risk, and that’s frightening. So yes, it wasn’t just a mood. It spilled over into my functioning and other people had to start paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a specific period lasting at least one full week where your mood was clearly and consistently elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in your energy or activity level, different enough from your usual self that it could be seen by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s the period I mean. I know I may be repeating myself a bit, but it was distinct enough that I don’t think I can explain it away as ordinary excitement or just being absorbed in work.\n\nFor most days in that stretch, I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up from the inside. I kept moving between the studio and the garden with this strong sense that everything was finally clicking into place, like the work and the space were part of one bigger plan that had suddenly become obvious to me. It felt very charged, very alive, and I had more drive to keep going with it than I normally would.\n\nBut it also came with this sharp irritability. If someone questioned what I was doing, or asked practical things like whether I’d sorted out invoices or whether the plans were realistic, I could snap at them in a way that really wasn’t typical for me. At the time I felt they were interrupting something important, but looking back, I can see that people around me would have noticed I was behaving differently.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of a specific week or longer when your mood was clearly more elevated or irritable than usual, along with much more energy or activity, different enough that it affected how you functioned and people around you noticed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / artist_or_related_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I felt unusually buoyant and lit up, like the studio and garden were finally clicking into place, but I could snap sharply at anyone who questioned my plans or asked about invoices.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, there was a stretch like that. I know I’ve been coming back to it, but it’s the clearest example because it really wasn’t my ordinary self.

For most days during that time, I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up, if that makes sense. I had this feeling that the studio and the garden were finally clicking into place together, like I could suddenly see how everything was meant to connect. It wasn’t just being pleased with an idea; it had this intensity to it, and I had much more energy around it than usual.

But I was also much more irritable. If anyone questioned my plans, or asked practical things—especially about invoices or whether I’d actually sorted certain things out—I could snap quite sharply. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, but looking back, I can see it was noticeable and not really like me. It did affect how I was functioning, because I was so caught up in that state that ordinary responsibilities felt secondary.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I think I’m here because things have gotten a little out of hand with how much I’ve taken on. I’m an artist, so I’m used to juggling projects and odd schedules and saying yes to opportunities when they come up, but lately I’ve been saying yes to everything—more client calls, more workshops, extra projects, even deciding the studio needs all these improvements right now.\n\nAt first it felt productive, like I was finally getting momentum, but now I’m looking at the calendar and realizing there’s just no realistic way to do it all properly. Deadlines are stacking up, people are expecting things from me, and honestly the mortgage is starting to feel like this pressure sitting on my chest. I keep thinking if I just push harder I can make it all work, but I’m not sure I can, and that’s why I thought I should come in before I make an even bigger mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods recently where your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or irritable, along with a lot more energy or activity than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Most days lately I’ve felt this kind of buoyant, almost lit-up feeling, like everything was finally coming together. I’d be in the studio or out in the garden and it felt like, “Of course, this is all clicking into place, I can do all of it, I can fix this, expand that, make it beautiful, make it work.” It wasn’t just being in a good mood, exactly—it was more charged than that.\n\nBut then if someone questioned me—like if they asked whether I really had time for something, or brought up invoices or practical things—I could get really sharp. Much sharper than I meant to be. It felt like they were interrupting something obvious and important, even if they were probably just being reasonable. So yes, unusually up, but also… irritable, definitely, when anyone pushed back on my plans.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods recently where your mood was elevated or irritable most of the day, nearly every day, along with a noticeable increase in your energy or activity level that lasted for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s a good way to put it. It wasn’t just a passing good mood, like having a nice morning or finishing a painting and feeling pleased. It was most days, and it would take over the whole day, really—this buoyant, lit-up feeling, like I had suddenly stepped into the right current and everything was moving with me.\n\nI’d be in the studio and feel like every idea connected to the next one, and then I’d go into the garden and feel the same thing there, like the studio and the garden were both finally clicking into place and I could see exactly what needed to happen. It felt obvious to me, almost embarrassingly obvious, that I should be doing all these things at once.\n\nBut if someone questioned it—if they asked whether I had time, or brought up invoices, or said something practical like, “Maybe wait on that,” I could snap. Sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. In the moment it felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, and then afterward I’d think, “Why did I say it like that?” So yes, elevated, but also irritable when people pushed against what I thought was the plan.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time recently when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy or activity, lasted for a full week or more without letting up?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than a week. It’s hard to pin down exactly because it didn’t feel like an “episode” at first, it felt like I was finally getting traction. But looking back, this has really been going on for about three months now—since I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio.\n\nThat was sort of the turning point, I think. Once that happened, everything started feeling urgent and possible at the same time, like I had to keep moving and keep building and keep taking things on to make it all work. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way since then. There might be quieter hours here and there, but the overall mood and energy—being so driven, so keyed into everything—has been there pretty consistently for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to be hospitalized or felt that your mood or behavior caused serious problems in your daily life, like work, relationships, or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. But… yes, it has caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nMy illustration work has really suffered. I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. And that’s not like me, or at least not like I want to be. Colleagues have started checking in on me, asking where things are, whether I’m okay, whether I need help getting back on track. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.\n\nAnd the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan. Now the mortgage is at risk, and I’m having to face that my mood and all this activity haven’t just been “creative momentum.” It’s affected my responsibilities in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your mood was elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in energy or activity, that lasted for at least four consecutive days and was different enough from your usual behavior that other people could clearly see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, people could see it. I mean, I don’t think I realized how visible it was at the time, because from inside it felt… wonderful at first, honestly. Like I was unusually buoyant, lit from underneath somehow. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden and it all felt like it was finally coming together, as if both places were part of one clear plan and I could suddenly see what needed doing.\n\nBut it wasn’t just feeling pleased or inspired. It was most of the day, most days, and I was much more energized and driven than I usually am. And if anyone questioned me—if they asked something practical, like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or if a plan really made sense—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I’m comfortable admitting. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, like they just couldn’t see what I could see.\n\nSo yes, it was different enough that other people noticed. I think they saw both sides of it—the brightness and the irritability.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific time when your mood and energy were so high that it was a clear change from how you usually feel, even compared to your typical creative bursts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I can picture it pretty clearly, actually. It wasn’t just one of my normal creative spells where I get absorbed in a piece and lose track of time for an afternoon. This felt bigger than that, and more… charged.\n\nMost days I had this buoyant feeling, like I was lit up from the inside. I’d walk between the studio and the garden and feel like, finally, finally, everything was clicking into place. The cottage, the studio, the planting, the work—it all seemed connected in this very obvious way, like there was a plan unfolding and I was the only one who could really see the whole shape of it.\n\nAnd I was much more intense than I usually am. I know I can get enthusiastic about projects, but this was different. If someone asked a practical question—like whether I’d handled invoices, or whether a plan was actually affordable, or even just tried to slow me down—I could snap at them. Sharply. Not just feel annoyed, but answer in this cutting way, like they were being foolish for not understanding what I was trying to do.\n\nAt the time I thought I was just energized and focused, but looking back, yes, it was a very clear change from my usual creative bursts.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific period when your mood was clearly more elevated or irritable than usual, along with much more energy or activity, lasting for at least seven days straight, and which people who know you well would say was not like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. It’s hard for me to put exact borders around it, because at the time I wasn’t thinking, “this is day one, this is day seven,” but there was definitely a stretch where, most days, I felt much more elevated than I normally do.\n\nI kept having this feeling of being unusually buoyant, almost lit up inside. I’d go back and forth between the studio and the garden and everything seemed to be clicking into place at once—the work, the space, the plans, all of it. It felt very vivid and obvious to me, like there was this larger pattern I could finally see.\n\nBut it wasn’t only pleasant. If someone interrupted that feeling by asking practical things—like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or whether a plan made sense, or if I was getting ahead of myself—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. At the time I felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, but looking back, I think people who know me would say I wasn’t quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy and activity, lasted for at least a full week and was clearly different from your usual behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can think of a time like that. I know I’ve sort of circled around it already, but it really does stand out because it wasn’t just me being in a good working rhythm or getting excited about a project.\n\nFor at least a week, most days I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up, like everything had suddenly become clear. I kept moving between the studio and the garden with this sense that they were finally clicking into place together, like the work and the space and the plans all had some obvious shape that I could see. It felt very energizing at the time, not like my normal pace.\n\nBut alongside that, I was much more irritable if anyone challenged me. If someone asked about practical things—like invoices, or whether the plans actually made sense—I could snap at them really sharply. I remember feeling as if they were interrupting something important, but looking back, the way I reacted was not like my usual self. People close to me would definitely have noticed that I was more intense than normal.\n\nDoctor: During this time when your mood and energy were elevated, did it ever get to the point where it was so intense that you had serious difficulty functioning in your daily life, others had to step in to help manage things, or you thought about or came close to needing hospitalization?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I didn’t end up in hospital, but it did start to affect real things in a way I couldn’t just brush off as “being busy” or “being inspired.”\n\nMy illustration deadlines slipped, which is very unlike me in that particular way. I might fuss over work or take too long perfecting something, but this was different—I was overcommitting, saying yes to things, starting plans, spending time and energy on all these connected ideas, and then the actual work I was meant to deliver wasn’t getting finished properly or on time.\n\nColleagues started checking in on me, not just casually, but in that careful way people do when they’re trying not to sound alarmed. Yes, I can. I’m sorry, I know I keep circling back to the same period, but it really is the clearest one.

There was at least a week—more than just a day or two—where most days I felt unusually buoyant, almost lit up. That’s the best way I can describe it. It wasn’t just a passing good mood, like having a nice morning or finishing a painting and feeling pleased. It was most days, and it would take over the whole day, really—this buoyant, lit-up feeling, like I had suddenly stepped into the right current and everything was moving with me.\n\nI’d be in the studio and feel like every idea connected to the next one, and then I’d go into the garden and feel the same thing there, like the studio and the garden were both finally clicking into place and I could see exactly what needed to happen. Once that happened, everything started feeling urgent and possible at the same time, like I had to keep moving and keep building and keep taking things on to make it all work. There might be quieter hours here and there, but the overall mood and energy—being so driven, so keyed into everything—has been there pretty consistently for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to be hospitalized or felt that your mood or behavior caused serious problems in your daily life, like work, relationships, or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. But… yes, it has caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nMy illustration work has really suffered. I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.\n\nAnd the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I mean, I don’t think I realized how visible it was at the time, because from inside it felt… wonderful at first, honestly. Like I was unusually buoyant, lit from underneath somehow. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden and it all felt like it was finally coming together, as if both places were part of one clear plan and I could suddenly see what needed doing.\n\nBut it wasn’t just feeling pleased or inspired. It was most of the day, most days, and I was much more energized and driven than I usually am. And if anyone questioned me—if they asked something practical, like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or if a plan really made sense—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I’m comfortable admitting. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, like they just couldn’t see what I could see.\n\nSo yes, it was different enough that other people noticed. I think they saw both sides of it—the brightness and the irritability.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific time when your mood and energy were so high that it was a clear change from how you usually feel, even compared to your typical creative bursts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I can picture it pretty clearly, actually. It wasn’t just one of my normal creative spells where I get absorbed in a piece and lose track of time for an afternoon. This felt bigger than that, and more… charged.\n\nMost days I had this buoyant feeling, like I was lit up from the inside. I’d walk between the studio and the garden and feel like, finally, finally, everything was clicking into place. The cottage, the studio, the planting, the work—it all seemed connected in this very obvious way, like there was a plan unfolding and I was the only one who could really see the whole shape of it.\n\nAnd I was much more intense than I usually am. I know I can get enthusiastic about projects, but this was different. If someone asked a practical question—like whether I’d handled invoices, or whether a plan was actually affordable, or even just tried to slow me down—I could snap at them. Sharply. Not just feel annoyed, but answer in this cutting way, like they were being foolish for not understanding what I was trying to do.\n\nAt the time I thought I was just energized and focused, but looking back, yes, it was a very clear change from my usual creative bursts.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific period when your mood was clearly more elevated or irritable than usual, along with much more energy or activity, lasting for at least seven days straight, and which people who know you well would say was not like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. It’s hard for me to put exact borders around it, because at the time I wasn’t thinking, “this is day one, this is day seven,” but there was definitely a stretch where, most days, I felt much more elevated than I normally do.\n\nI kept having this feeling of being unusually buoyant, almost lit up inside. I’d go back and forth between the studio and the garden and everything seemed to be clicking into place at once—the work, the space, the plans, all of it. It felt very vivid and obvious to me, like there was this larger pattern I could finally see.\n\nBut it wasn’t only pleasant. If someone interrupted that feeling by asking practical things—like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or whether a plan made sense, or if I was getting ahead of myself—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. At the time I felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, but looking back, I think people who know me would say I wasn’t quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy and activity, lasted for at least a full week and was clearly different from your usual behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can think of a time like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I can think of that time. I know I’ve probably described it already, but it’s the one that stands out because it felt so different from my normal way of being.

For most days during that stretch, I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up, really. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden, and it felt as if everything was finally clicking into place. Not just in the ordinary “I’ve had a good idea” sort of way, but like the studio, the garden, the work, the plans I had were all connected and suddenly obvious to me. I had much more energy around it than I usually do, and I was very taken up with it.

But at the same time, I was much more irritable. If anyone questioned what I was doing, or asked me about practical things—especially invoices, or whether the plans were realistic—I could snap at them quite sharply. At the time I felt they were getting in the way of something important, but looking back, yes, I think people who know me well would have seen it as not quite like me.

It felt obvious to me, almost embarrassingly obvious, that I should be doing all these things at once.\n\nBut if someone questioned it—if they asked whether I had time, or brought up invoices, or said something practical like, “Maybe wait on that,” I could snap. Sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. Once that happened, everything started feeling urgent and possible at the same time, like I had to keep moving and keep building and keep taking things on to make it all work. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way since then. There might be quieter hours here and there, but the overall mood and energy—being so driven, so keyed into everything—has been there pretty consistently for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to be hospitalized or felt that your mood or behavior caused serious problems in your daily life, like work, relationships, or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. But… yes, it has caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nMy illustration work has really suffered. I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.\n\nAnd the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan. Now the mortgage is at risk, and I’m having to face that my mood and all this activity haven’t just been “creative momentum.” It’s affected my responsibilities in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your mood was elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in energy or activity, that lasted for at least four consecutive days and was different enough from your usual behavior that other people could clearly see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, people could see it. I mean, I don’t think I realized how visible it was at the time, because from inside it felt… wonderful at first, honestly. Like I was unusually buoyant, lit from underneath somehow. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden and it all felt like it was finally coming together, as if both places were part of one clear plan and I could suddenly see what needed doing.\n\nBut it wasn’t just feeling pleased or inspired. It was most of the day, most days, and I was much more energized and driven than I usually am. And if anyone questioned me—if they asked something practical, like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or if a plan really made sense—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I’m comfortable admitting. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, like they just couldn’t see what I could see.\n\nSo yes, it was different enough that other people noticed. I think they saw both sides of it—the brightness and the irritability.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific time when your mood and energy were so high that it was a clear change from how you usually feel, even compared to your typical creative bursts?\n\nPatient: Yes. This felt bigger than that, and more… charged.\n\nMost days I had this buoyant feeling, like I was lit up from the inside. I’d walk between the studio and the garden and feel like, finally, finally, everything was clicking into place. The cottage, the studio, the planting, the work—it all seemed connected in this very obvious way, like there was a plan unfolding and I was the only one who could really see the whole shape of it.\n\nAnd I was much more intense than I usually am. I know I can get enthusiastic about projects, but this was different. There might be quieter hours here and there, but the overall mood and energy—being so driven, so keyed into everything—has been there pretty consistently for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to be hospitalized or felt that your mood or behavior caused serious problems in your daily life, like work, relationships, or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. But… yes, it has caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nMy illustration work has really suffered. I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. And that’s not like me, or at least not like I want to be. Colleagues have started checking in on me, asking where things are, whether I’m okay, whether I need help getting back on track. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.\n\nAnd the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan. Now the mortgage is at risk, and I’m having to face that my mood and all this activity haven’t just been “creative momentum.” It’s affected my responsibilities in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your mood was elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in energy or activity, that lasted for at least four consecutive days and was different enough from your usual behavior that other people could clearly see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, people could see it. I mean, I don’t think I realized how visible it was at the time, because from inside it felt… wonderful at first, honestly. Like I was unusually buoyant, lit from underneath somehow. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden and it all felt like it was finally coming together, as if both places were part of one clear plan and I could suddenly see what needed doing.\n\nBut it wasn’t just feeling pleased or inspired. It was most of the day, most days, and I was much more energized and driven than I usually am. And if anyone questioned me—if they asked something practical, like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or if a plan really made sense—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I’m comfortable admitting. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, like they just couldn’t see what I could see.\n\nSo yes, it was different enough that other people noticed. I think they saw both sides of it—the brightness and the irritability.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific time when your mood and energy were so high that it was a clear change from how you usually feel, even compared to your typical creative bursts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I can picture it pretty clearly, actually. It wasn’t just one of my normal creative spells where I get absorbed in a piece and lose track of time for an afternoon. This felt bigger than that, and more… charged.\n\nMost days I had this buoyant feeling, like I was lit up from the inside. I’d walk between the studio and the garden and feel like, finally, finally, everything was clicking into place. The cottage, the studio, the planting, the work—it all seemed connected in this very obvious way, like there was a plan unfolding and I was the only one who could really see the whole shape of it.\n\nAnd I was much more intense than I usually am. I know I can get enthusiastic about projects, but this was different. If someone asked a practical question—like whether I’d handled invoices, or whether a plan was actually affordable, or even just tried to slow me down—I could snap at them. Sharply. Not just feel annoyed, but answer in this cutting way, like they were being foolish for not understanding what I was trying to do.\n\nAt the time I thought I was just energized and focused, but looking back, yes, it was a very clear change from my usual creative bursts.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific period when your mood was clearly more elevated or irritable than usual, along with much more energy or activity, lasting for at least seven days straight, and which people who know you well would say was not like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. It’s hard for me to put exact borders around it, because at the time I wasn’t thinking, “this is day one, this is day seven,” but there was definitely a stretch where, most days, I felt much more elevated than I normally do.\n\nI kept having this feeling of being unusually buoyant, almost lit up inside. I’d go back and forth between the studio and the garden and everything seemed to be clicking into place at once—the work, the space, the plans, all of it. If someone interrupted that feeling by asking practical things—like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or whether a plan made sense, or if I was getting ahead of myself—I could snap at them very sharply. I keep thinking if I just push harder I can make it all work, but I’m not sure I can, and that’s why I thought I should come in before I make an even bigger mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods recently where your mood was unusually high, euphoric, or irritable, along with a lot more energy or activity than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Most days lately I’ve felt this kind of buoyant, almost lit-up feeling, like everything was finally coming together. I’d be in the studio or out in the garden and it felt like, “Of course, this is all clicking into place, I can do all of it, I can fix this, expand that, make it beautiful, make it work.” It wasn’t just being in a good mood, exactly—it was more charged than that.\n\nBut then if someone questioned me—like if they asked whether I really had time for something, or brought up invoices or practical things—I could get really sharp. Much sharper than I meant to be. It felt like they were interrupting something obvious and important, even if they were probably just being reasonable. So yes, unusually up, but also… irritable, definitely, when anyone pushed back on my plans.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods recently where your mood was elevated or irritable most of the day, nearly every day, along with a noticeable increase in your energy or activity level that lasted for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s a good way to put it. It wasn’t just a passing good mood, like having a nice morning or finishing a painting and feeling pleased. It was most days, and it would take over the whole day, really—this buoyant, lit-up feeling, like I had suddenly stepped into the right current and everything was moving with me.\n\nI’d be in the studio and feel like every idea connected to the next one, and then I’d go into the garden and feel the same thing there, like the studio and the garden were both finally clicking into place and I could see exactly what needed to happen. It felt obvious to me, almost embarrassingly obvious, that I should be doing all these things at once.\n\nBut if someone questioned it—if they asked whether I had time, or brought up invoices, or said something practical like, “Maybe wait on that,” I could snap. Sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. In the moment it felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, and then afterward I’d think, “Why did I say it like that?” So yes, elevated, but also irritable when people pushed against what I thought was the plan.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time recently when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy or activity, lasted for a full week or more without letting up?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than a week. It’s hard to pin down exactly because it didn’t feel like an “episode” at first, it felt like I was finally getting traction. But looking back, this has really been going on for about three months now—since I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio.\n\nThat was sort of the turning point, I think. Once that happened, everything started feeling urgent and possible at the same time, like I had to keep moving and keep building and keep taking things on to make it all work. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way since then. There might be quieter hours here and there, but the overall mood and energy—being so driven, so keyed into everything—has been there pretty consistently for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to be hospitalized or felt that your mood or behavior caused serious problems in your daily life, like work, relationships, or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. But… yes, it has caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nMy illustration work has really suffered. I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. And that’s not like me, or at least not like I want to be. Colleagues have started checking in on me, asking where things are, whether I’m okay, whether I need help getting back on track. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.\n\nAnd the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan. Now the mortgage is at risk, and I’m having to face that my mood and all this activity haven’t just been “creative momentum.” It’s affected my responsibilities in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your mood was elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in energy or activity, that lasted for at least four consecutive days and was different enough from your usual behavior that other people could clearly see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, people could see it. I mean, I don’t think I realized how visible it was at the time, because from inside it felt… wonderful at first, honestly. Like I was unusually buoyant, lit from underneath somehow. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden and it all felt like it was finally coming together, as if both places were part of one clear plan and I could suddenly see what needed doing.\n\nBut it wasn’t just feeling pleased or inspired. It was most of the day, most days, and I was much more energized and driven than I usually am. And if anyone questioned me—if they asked something practical, like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or if a plan really made sense—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I’m comfortable admitting. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, like they just couldn’t see what I could see.\n\nSo yes, it was different enough that other people noticed. I think they saw both sides of it—the brightness and the irritability.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific time when your mood and energy were so high that it was a clear change from how you usually feel, even compared to your typical creative bursts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I can picture it pretty clearly, actually. It wasn’t just one of my normal creative spells where I get absorbed in a piece and lose track of time for an afternoon. This felt bigger than that, and more… charged.\n\nMost days I had this buoyant feeling, like I was lit up from the inside. I’d walk between the studio and the garden and feel like, finally, finally, everything was clicking into place. The cottage, the studio, the planting, the work—it all seemed connected in this very obvious way, like there was a plan unfolding and I was the only one who could really see the whole shape of it.\n\nAnd I was much more intense than I usually am. I know I can get enthusiastic about projects, but this was different. If someone asked a practical question—like whether I’d handled invoices, or whether a plan was actually affordable, or even just tried to slow me down—I could snap at them. Sharply. Not just feel annoyed, but answer in this cutting way, like they were being foolish for not understanding what I was trying to do.\n\nAt the time I thought I was just energized and focused, but looking back, yes, it was a very clear change from my usual creative bursts.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific period when your mood was clearly more elevated or irritable than usual, along with much more energy or activity, lasting for at least seven days straight, and which people who know you well would say was not like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. It’s hard for me to put exact borders around it, because at the time I wasn’t thinking, “this is day one, this is day seven,” but there was definitely a stretch where, most days, I felt much more elevated than I normally do.\n\nI kept having this feeling of being unusually buoyant, almost lit up inside. I’d go back and forth between the studio and the garden and everything seemed to be clicking into place at once—the work, the space, the plans, all of it. It felt very vivid and obvious to me, like there was this larger pattern I could finally see.\n\nBut it wasn’t only pleasant. If someone interrupted that feeling by asking practical things—like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or whether a plan made sense, or if I was getting ahead of myself—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. At the time I felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, but looking back, I think people who know me would say I wasn’t quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy and activity, lasted for at least a full week and was clearly different from your usual behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can think of a time like that. I know I’ve sort of circled around it already, but it really does stand out because it wasn’t just me being in a good working rhythm or getting excited about a project.\n\nFor at least a week, most days I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up, like everything had suddenly become clear. I kept moving between the studio and the garden with this sense that they were finally clicking into place together, like the work and the space and the plans all had some obvious shape that I could see. It felt very energizing at the time, not like my normal pace.\n\nBut alongside that, I was much more irritable if anyone challenged me. If someone asked about practical things—like invoices, or whether the plans actually made sense—I could snap at them really sharply. I remember feeling as if they were interrupting something important, but looking back, the way I reacted was not like my usual self. People close to me would definitely have noticed that I was more intense than normal.\n\nDoctor: During this time when your mood and energy were elevated, did it ever get to the point where it was so intense that you had serious difficulty functioning in your daily life, others had to step in to help manage things, or you thought about or came close to needing hospitalization?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I didn’t end up in hospital, but it did start to affect real things in a way I couldn’t just brush off as “being busy” or “being inspired.”\n\nMy illustration deadlines slipped, which is very unlike me in that particular way. I might fuss over work or take too long perfecting something, but this was different—I was overcommitting, saying yes to things, starting plans, spending time and energy on all these connected ideas, and then the actual work I was meant to deliver wasn’t getting finished properly or on time.\n\nColleagues started checking in on me, not just casually, but in that careful way people do when they’re trying not to sound alarmed. And financially… that’s the part I feel most ashamed about. I spent money I really needed to keep the studio secure, because at the time it felt justified, like it was all part of building something important. But now the mortgage is at risk, and that’s frightening. So yes, it wasn’t just a mood. It spilled over into my functioning and other people had to start paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a specific period lasting at least one full week where your mood was clearly and consistently elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in your energy or activity level, different enough from your usual self that it could be seen by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s the period I mean. I know I may be repeating myself a bit, but it was distinct enough that I don’t think I can explain it away as ordinary excitement or just being absorbed in work.\n\nFor most days in that stretch, I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up from the inside. I kept moving between the studio and the garden with this strong sense that everything was finally clicking into place, like the work and the space were part of one bigger plan that had suddenly become obvious to me. It felt very charged, very alive, and I had more drive to keep going with it than I normally would.\n\nBut it also came with this sharp irritability. If someone questioned what I was doing, or asked practical things like whether I’d sorted out invoices or whether the plans were realistic, I could snap at them in a way that really wasn’t typical for me. At the time I felt they were interrupting something important, but looking back, I can see that people around me would have noticed I was behaving differently.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific week or longer when your mood was clearly more elevated or irritable than usual, along with much more energy or activity, different enough that it affected how you functioned and people around you noticed?\n\nPatient: Yes, there was a stretch like that. I know I’ve been coming back to it, but it’s the clearest example because it really wasn’t my ordinary self.\n\nFor most days during that time, I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up, if that makes sense. I had this feeling that the studio and the garden were finally clicking into place together, like I could suddenly see how everything was meant to connect. It wasn’t just being pleased with an idea; it had this intensity to it, and I had much more energy around it than usual.\n\nBut I was also much more irritable. If anyone questioned my plans, or asked practical things—especially about invoices or whether I’d actually sorted certain things out—I could snap quite sharply. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, but looking back, I can see it was noticeable and not really like me. It did affect how I was functioning, because I was so caught up in that state that ordinary responsibilities felt secondary.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time when your mood was clearly more elevated or irritable than usual, along with much more energy or activity, lasting for at least a full week, and which people who know you well would say was not like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can. If someone asked practical questions, especially about invoices or whether my plans actually made sense, I could snap at them quite sharply. Yes, I can confirm that. It was a specific stretch, at least a full week, where I really wasn’t in my usual state.

Most days I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit from the inside, if that doesn’t sound too dramatic. It was most days, and it would take over the whole day, really—this buoyant, lit-up feeling, like I had suddenly stepped into the right current and everything was moving with me.\n\nI’d be in the studio and feel like every idea connected to the next one, and then I’d go into the garden and feel the same thing there, like the studio and the garden were both finally clicking into place and I could see exactly what needed to happen. This felt bigger than that, and more… charged.\n\nMost days I had this buoyant feeling, like I was lit up from the inside. I’d walk between the studio and the garden and feel like, finally, finally, everything was clicking into place. The cottage, the studio, the planting, the work—it all seemed connected in this very obvious way, like there was a plan unfolding and I was the only one who could really see the whole shape of it.\n\nAnd I was much more intense than I usually am. I know I can get enthusiastic about projects, but this was different. If someone asked a practical question—like whether I’d handled invoices, or whether a plan was actually affordable, or even just tried to slow me down—I could snap at them. Sharply. It’s hard for me to put exact borders around it, because at the time I wasn’t thinking, “this is day one, this is day seven,” but there was definitely a stretch where, most days, I felt much more elevated than I normally do.\n\nI kept having this feeling of being unusually buoyant, almost lit up inside. I’d go back and forth between the studio and the garden and everything seemed to be clicking into place at once—the work, the space, the plans, all of it. It felt very vivid and obvious to me, like there was this larger pattern I could finally see.\n\nBut it wasn’t only pleasant. If someone interrupted that feeling by asking practical things—like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or whether a plan made sense, or if I was getting ahead of myself—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. At the time I felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, but looking back, I think people who know me would say I wasn’t quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy and activity, lasted for at least a full week and was clearly different from your usual behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can think of a time like that. I know I’ve sort of circled around it already, but it really does stand out because it wasn’t just me being in a good working rhythm or getting excited about a project.\n\nFor at least a week, most days I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up, like everything had suddenly become clear. I kept moving between the studio and the garden with this sense that they were finally clicking into place together, like the work and the space and the plans all had some obvious shape that I could see. It felt very energizing at the time, not like my normal pace.\n\nBut alongside that, I was much more irritable if anyone challenged me. If someone asked about practical things—like invoices, or whether the plans actually made sense—I could snap at them really sharply. Yes, I can recall that. I know I’ve been circling around the same period, but yes, there was definitely a distinct spell of days where I wasn’t my usual self.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes—what I remember most clearly was the activity, the constant need to be doing something. It was like there was this humming current in me from the morning onward, and sitting quietly felt almost impossible.

I’d go into the studio meaning to do one small thing, and then suddenly I’d be rearranging half the space, moving tables, sorting supplies, pulling materials out, making lists, then abandoning the lists to draft proposals or email people. I kept fussing with things—paint, tools, papers, seed trays, whatever was in front of me—because I felt like if I stopped, I’d lose the thread of it all.

It wasn’t just normal productivity. It had this driven quality to it, like I had to keep acting on every idea as it came up.

It was most days, and it would take over the whole day, really—this buoyant, lit-up feeling, like I had suddenly stepped into the right current and everything was moving with me.\n\nI’d be in the studio and feel like every idea connected to the next one, and then I’d go into the garden and feel the same thing there, like the studio and the garden were both finally clicking into place and I could see exactly what needed to happen.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it racing thoughts, exactly. I mean, there were a lot of ideas, but I don’t know that I experienced it as my thoughts going so fast I couldn’t keep up with them.

It was more like I’d get very absorbed in one idea and then another would sort of branch off from it, especially around the studio or the garden. I could spend ages thinking through how one thing connected to another, and I probably got a bit carried away with that. But if you’re asking whether my mind felt out of control or like thoughts were flying past me faster than I could catch them… I don’t know. I don’t think I noticed it in that way.

I suppose other people might have thought I was jumping around a bit, because I’d move from one plan to another, but from the inside it felt more like enthusiasm and momentum than racing. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes to that if I’m not certain.

I kept telling myself I was being productive because I was taking on so much and seeing all these possibilities, but the actual deadlines—the ones with clients waiting, real deliverables—those have slipped. And that’s not like me, or at least not like I want to be. Colleagues have started checking in on me, asking where things are, whether I’m okay, whether I need help getting back on track. They’re being kind, but it’s embarrassing, because I can hear the concern underneath it.\n\nAnd the mortgage… that’s the part that scares me when I let myself actually look at it. I overcommitted. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan. Now the mortgage is at risk, and I’m having to face that my mood and all this activity haven’t just been “creative momentum.” It’s affected my responsibilities in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your mood was elevated or irritable, along with a noticeable increase in energy or activity, that lasted for at least four consecutive days and was different enough from your usual behavior that other people could clearly see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, people could see it. I mean, I don’t think I realized how visible it was at the time, because from inside it felt… wonderful at first, honestly. Like I was unusually buoyant, lit from underneath somehow. I’d be moving between the studio and the garden and it all felt like it was finally coming together, as if both places were part of one clear plan and I could suddenly see what needed doing.\n\nBut it wasn’t just feeling pleased or inspired. It was most of the day, most days, and I was much more energized and driven than I usually am. And if anyone questioned me—if they asked something practical, like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or if a plan really made sense—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I’m comfortable admitting. At the time it felt like they were interrupting something important, like they just couldn’t see what I could see.\n\nSo yes, it was different enough that other people noticed. I think they saw both sides of it—the brightness and the irritability.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific time when your mood and energy were so high that it was a clear change from how you usually feel, even compared to your typical creative bursts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’d walk between the studio and the garden and feel like, finally, finally, everything was clicking into place. Not just feel annoyed, but answer in this cutting way, like they were being foolish for not understanding what I was trying to do.\n\nAt the time I thought I was just energized and focused, but looking back, yes, it was a very clear change from my usual creative bursts.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific period when your mood was clearly more elevated or irritable than usual, along with much more energy or activity, lasting for at least seven days straight, and which people who know you well would say was not like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I can. It’s hard for me to put exact borders around it, because at the time I wasn’t thinking, “this is day one, this is day seven,” but there was definitely a stretch where, most days, I felt much more elevated than I normally do.\n\nI kept having this feeling of being unusually buoyant, almost lit up inside. I’d go back and forth between the studio and the garden and everything seemed to be clicking into place at once—the work, the space, the plans, all of it. It felt very vivid and obvious to me, like there was this larger pattern I could finally see.\n\nBut it wasn’t only pleasant. If someone interrupted that feeling by asking practical things—like whether I’d dealt with invoices, or whether a plan made sense, or if I was getting ahead of myself—I could snap at them very sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. At the time I felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, but looking back, I think people who know me would say I wasn’t quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy and activity, lasted for at least a full week and was clearly different from your usual behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, I can think of a time like that. I know I’ve sort of circled around it already, but it really does stand out because it wasn’t just me being in a good working rhythm or getting excited about a project.\n\nFor at least a week, most days I felt unusually buoyant—almost lit up, like everything had suddenly become clear. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that did happen, though it’s a little embarrassing to say out loud now.

At the time, I became very convinced that my illustration work wasn’t just useful or good, but that it could become this kind of missing visual language for scientific journals. Like, I remember thinking that science had been explaining itself in these dry, limited ways for decades, and that what I was doing could somehow bridge that gap — make things visible in a way no one else had quite managed.

I felt oddly certain that I was the one person who could pull it off, like I had this particular combination of visual sense and understanding and patience that other people didn’t have. I can see now that sounds grandiose, but at the time it felt completely clear and important, almost obvious.

So yes, unusually up, but also… irritable, definitely, when anyone pushed back on my plans.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct periods recently where your mood was elevated or irritable most of the day, nearly every day, along with a noticeable increase in your energy or activity level that lasted for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s a good way to put it. It wasn’t just a passing good mood, like having a nice morning or finishing a painting and feeling pleased. It was most days, and it would take over the whole day, really—this buoyant, lit-up feeling, like I had suddenly stepped into the right current and everything was moving with me.\n\nI’d be in the studio and feel like every idea connected to the next one, and then I’d go into the garden and feel the same thing there, like the studio and the garden were both finally clicking into place and I could see exactly what needed to happen. It felt obvious to me, almost embarrassingly obvious, that I should be doing all these things at once.\n\nBut if someone questioned it—if they asked whether I had time, or brought up invoices, or said something practical like, “Maybe wait on that,” I could snap. Sharply. Much more sharply than I usually would. In the moment it felt like they were slowing me down or missing the point, and then afterward I’d think, “Why did I say it like that?” So yes, elevated, but also irritable when people pushed against what I thought was the plan.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time recently when this elevated or irritable mood, along with the increased energy or activity, lasted for a full week or more without letting up?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than a week. Once that happened, everything started feeling urgent and possible at the same time, like I had to keep moving and keep building and keep taking things on to make it all work. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way since then. There might be quieter hours here and there, but the overall mood and energy—being so driven, so keyed into everything—has been there pretty consistently for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to be hospitalized or felt that your mood or behavior caused serious problems in your daily life, like work, relationships, or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been hospitalized for it, no. But… yes, it has caused problems. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nMy illustration work has really suffered. I spent money I should have kept aside to keep the cottage-studio secure, because in the moment it all felt necessary and connected to the bigger plan.
